On deformations of Azumaya algebras with quadratic pair Thanks: The second author was supported by the project “Group schemes, root systems, and related representations” founded by the European Union - NextGenerationEU through Romania’s National Recovery and Resilience Plan (PNRR) call no. PNRR-III-C9-2023-I8, Project CF159/31.07.2023, and coordinated by the Ministry of Research, Innovation and Digitalization (MCID) of Romania.
Eoin Mackall, Cameron Ruether

Abstract
. We construct a tangent-obstruction theory for Azumaya algebras equipped with a quadratic pair. Under the assumption that either 2 2 is a global unit or the algebra is of degree 2 2 , we show how the deformation theory of these objects reduces to the deformation theory of the underlying Azumaya algebra. Namely, if the underlying Azumaya algebra has unobstructed deformations then so does the quadratic pair. On the other hand, in the purely characteristic 2 2 setting, we construct an Azumaya algebra with unobstructed deformations which can be equipped with a quadratic pair such that the associated triple has obstructed deformations. Our example is a biquaternion Azumaya algebra on an Igusa surface. Independently from the above results, we also introduce a new obstruction for quadratic pairs, existing only in characteristic 2, which is intermediate to both the strong and weak obstructions that were recently introduced by Gille, Neher, and the second named author. This intermediate obstruction characterizes when a canonical extension of the Lie algebra sheaf of the automorphism group scheme of some quadratic triple is split.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 14D15, Secondary: 11E99, 16H05, 16W10, 20G10, 20G35

Introduction

Background

Constructing moduli spaces and understanding their geometry has been a driving force behind the development of much of modern algebraic geometry. Using Grothendieck’s functorial perspective, the deformation theory of objects that are parametrized by a moduli space is a natural subject arising from the study of the local smoothness of that moduli space. A moduli space fails to be smooth at a point when the object which that point parameterizes fails to deform in some way.

A landmark example of such phenomena comes from an Igusa surface
[
Igu55
]
. An Igusa surface is a scheme, existing over a field
k
of characteristic
p>0
, which can be realized as the quotient of a product of two elliptic curves, with at least one being ordinary, by a free action of
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
. While it’s known that the Picard scheme of any variety in characteristic
0
is smooth, the Picard scheme of an Igusa surface is everywhere nonreduced. As a consequence, given any line bundle on such a surface, we can find a lifting of that line bundle to a thickening of the Igusa surface which has obstructed deformations to some further infinitesimal thickening.

Even when an object is not obviously parametrized by a moduli space, studying the deformation theory of that object can often shed light on how the object varies in families. For a modern example making use of such analysis, we point to
[
Zda21
]
where Zdanowicz uses the deformation theory of equivariant bundles to show that certain Fano varieties in characteristic
p>0
which violate Kodaira vanishing do not lift to any ring in which
p\neq 0
, i.e., any ring which does not contain
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
as a subring. For an example in the theme of this paper, we point to de Jong’s proof of the period index conjecture for the function fields of surfaces
[
dJ04
]
which relies crucially on the deformation theory of Azumaya algebras.

The deformation theory of a
\mathbf{G}
-torsor, for a group scheme
\mathbf{G}
over a base
S
, was worked out in great generality by Illusie, see
[
Ill72
, Theorem 2.6]
. If one restricts themselves to group schemes
\mathbf{G}
which are smooth over
S
, then the theory reduces to one of the underlying sheaf of Lie algebras of
\mathbf{G}
. In particular, for an infinitesimal thickening of schemes
X\subset X^{\prime}
defined by a square-zero ideal
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
, a
\mathbf{G}
–torsor
\mathscr{H}
on
X
has an obstruction class in
\rH^{2}(X,\mathfrak{Lie}_{\mathbf{G}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{J})
which vanishes if and only if a deformation
\mathscr{H}^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{H}
exists on
X^{\prime}
. When such a deformation exists, one further knows that all such deformations form an affine space under
\rH^{1}(X,\mathfrak{Lie}_{\mathbf{G}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{J})
.

An important question is then: for a given
\mathbf{G}
, under what conditions does one have the vanishing, or nonvanishing, of these cohomological obstruction classes? Demonstrations of nonvanishing obstructions are typically rarer and, therefore, of more interest. De Clerq, Florence, and Lucchini Arteche have anlayzed this problem for deformations of vector bundles from a scheme
X
to schemes
\mathbf{W}_{r}(X)
of Witt vectors of
X
,
[
DCFLA23
]
. They show that, for integers
n,m
with
2\leq m\leq n-2
, the tautological vector bundle on a Grassmannian
\mathbf{Gr}(m,n)
over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
has obstructed deformations to
\mathbf{W}_{2}(\mathbf{Gr}(m,n))
. This work should be compared with that of Zdanowicz
[
Zda21
, Section 7.3]
who uses a similar result to construct torsors with obstructed deformations under semisimple linear algebraic group schemes
\mathbf{G}
of classical type.

The standard passage from finite rank vector bundles to Azumaya algebras sends a vector bundle to its endomorphism algebra sheaf. If the endomorphism algebra sheaf of a vector bundle has obstructed deformations then the vector bundle is necessarily also obstructed. However, it is easy to see that the property of having obstructed deformations is not always preserved in the other direction. One can take any line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on an Igusa surface
X
which has obstructed deformations relative to some thickening of
X
. Then, the endomorphism algebra
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{L})\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}
clearly admits deformations. In examples such as this, we say that
\mathcal{L}
is
relatively obstructed
compared to its endomorphism algebra
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{L})
. Inversely, we would say that line bundles are
relatively unobstructed
compared to their endomorphism algebras for a given infinitesimal thickening if, whenever
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{L})
deforms then so must
\mathcal{L}
. We will use this terminology, relatively obstructed or relatively unobstructed, to describe how various functors interact with obstructions to deformations.

Deformations of Azumaya algebras with quadratic pair

In this paper we investigate whether Azumaya algebras with quadratic pair are relatively obstructed compared to their underlying Azumaya algebra. Quadratic pairs on central simple algebras were introduced in
[
KMRT98
]
as a tool that allows one to solve problems related to quadratic forms and linear algebraic groups of type
D
in characteristic
2
. Namely, quadratic pairs provide a way to describe, in any characteristic, any group of type
D_{n}
, excluding
D_{4}
and trialty, as the automorphism group of a central simple algebra with quadratic pair. This construction was recently extended to Azumaya algebras in
[
CF15
]
and studied further in
[
GNR25
]
.

In more detail, a quadratic pair
(\sigma,f)
on an Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{A}
on an arbitrary scheme
X
consists of an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–linear orthogonal involution
\sigma\colon\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
and a linear map
f\colon\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
on the subsheaf
\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}\subset\mathcal{A}
of sections invariant under
\sigma
, which satisfies certain properties (see
Section

1.A.iii
for more). The map
f
is called a semi-trace and we often call such a tuple
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
a quadratic triple.

Quadratic pairs on degree
n
Azumaya algebras are in correspondence with
\mathbf{PGO}_{n}
–torsors and, therefore, their deformation theory is covered by the results from
[
Ill72
]
. Using this theory, we address the following relative deformation question: is there an Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{A}
with quadratic pair
(\sigma,f)
such that the quadratic triple
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
has obstructed deformations but such that the deformations of
\mathcal{A}
, simply considered as an Azumaya algebra, are unobstructed?

We work in the following framework. Let
(C,\mathfrak{m}_{C})
and
(C^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}_{C^{\prime}})
be two local Artinian
k
–algebras whose residue fields are also
k
. Let
C^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow C
be a surjection given by an ideal
J\subseteq\mathfrak{m}_{C^{\prime}}
such that
\mathfrak{m}_{C^{\prime}}\cdot J=0
; note that this implies
J^{2}=0
. Such a surjection is called a small extension. We consider a
k
-scheme
X
and the associated schemes
X_{C}=X\times_{k}C
and
X_{C^{\prime}}=X\times_{k}C^{\prime}
. The map
X_{C}\hookrightarrow X_{C^{\prime}}
associated to
C^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow C
is then a square-zero (first order) thickening of schemes.

Our first result regarding the relative obstructions of quadratic pairs is a positive statement, showing that vanishing of obstructions for the quadratic pair follows from vanishing of obstructions of the underlying Azumaya algebra in two notable special cases (our
Theorem

3.3
).

Theorem A
.

Let
X_{C}\rightarrow X_{C^{\prime}}
be a square-zero thickening of schemes associated to a small extension of local Artinian
k
–algebras as above. Let
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
be an Azumaya algebra with quadratic pair on
X_{C}
.

Then, there exists a deformation
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},f^{\prime})
on
X_{C^{\prime}}
of the quadratic triple
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
if and only if there exists a deformation
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
of the underlying Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{A}
under either of the following two hypothesis:

(i)

assuming either the characteristic of
k
is not
2
, i.e.
\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2
;

(ii)

or assuming
\mathrm{deg}(\mathcal{A})=2
.

Note that, in the above statement, the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
appearing in the deformation
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},f^{\prime})
is not necessarily the same as the deformation
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
of just the algebra
\mathcal{A}
.

In the opposite extreme, we construct an example satisfying neither of the above hypothesis
(i)
or
(ii)
where such relative obstructions really do exist. Our example makes use of the existing obstructions to deforming line bundles on an Igusa surface. Specifically, let
X
be an Igusa surface defined over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
2
. Let
\mu_{2}=\Spec(k[x]/(x^{2}))
,
\tau_{3}=\Spec(k[x]/(x^{3}))
, with the canonical closed embedding
\mu_{2}\rightarrow\tau_{3}
. We then have morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mu_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\tau_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where the top horizontal map is a square-zero infinitesimal thickening and the slanted arrows are the canonical projection maps. We show (in
Theorem

4.5
below) the following:

Theorem B
.

There exists an Igusa surface
X
(gotten as the quotient of any two ordinary elliptic curves) and a quadratic triple
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
on
X_{\mu_{2}}
such that

(i)

the quadratic triple
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
does not deform as a quadratic triple to
X_{\tau_{3}}
;

(ii)

the Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{A}
on
X_{\mu_{2}}
does deform to
X_{\tau_{3}}
(the algebra
\mathcal{A}
is even unobstructed).

The quadratic pair appearing in the theorem is a canonically constructed pair on a biquaternion algebra
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
on
X_{\mu_{2}}
. The quadratic pair comes from the equivalence of stacks shown in
[
GNR24
]
which reflects the exceptional isomorphism between the Dynkin types
A_{1}\times A_{1}
and
D_{2}
. Because the quadratic pair comes from this equivalence, it will deform if and only if both
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
deform. However, we construct
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in such a way to ensure that at least one of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
or
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
has obstructed deformations. We can do this by exploiting the fact that the Igusa surface
X
has line bundles with obstructed deformations.

More precisely, we start with an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\rightarrow 0

where
\mathcal{L}
is a particular line bundle on
X
and
\mathcal{V}
is a rank
2
vector bundle such that we have an explicit cocycle description for the quaternion algebra
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{V})
. The challenge is to find an Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{P}
on
X
such that
(\mathcal{Q}\otimes\mathcal{P})(X)=k
. We use this condition critically, along with either
Corollary

1.22
or
Theorem

3.5
below, to ensure that any nontrivial obstruction we create vanishes once the quadratic pair is forgotten. That is to say, we prove (using that
K_{X}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X})
that this condition implies that the canonical map on the cohomology of Lie algebra sheaves

\mathrm{H}^{2}(X,\mathfrak{pgo}_{(\mathcal{Q}\otimes\mathcal{P},\sigma,f)})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{2}(X,\mathfrak{pgl}_{\mathcal{Q}\otimes\mathcal{P}}),

is identically zero. Although we believe that this condition is most likely generically satisfied, most of the examples that we could produce did not have this condition. The algebra
\mathcal{P}
with these properties which we use is constructed from a canonical
\mathbf{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
-torsor over
X
. Since
\mathbf{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{2})=\mathbf{PGL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
, it is therefore also neutral, so
\mathcal{P}\cong\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}(\mathcal{W})
for some rank
2
vector bundle
\mathcal{W}
on
X
as well.

Next, we argue that at least one of
\mathcal{Q}
or
\mathcal{P}
must have a deformation to
X_{\mu_{2}}
with obstructed deformations to
X_{\tau_{3}}
. We do this by showing that simple rank
2
vector bundles on
X
are relatively unobstructed compared to their endomorphism algebra sheaves. We then show that collective deformations of the (simple) vector bundles
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
underlying
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
have determinant bundles which admit deformations in independent directions. We show that at least one of these directions corresponds to a line bundle with obstructed deformations to
X_{\tau_{3}}
. All together, this allows us to prove
Theorem

B
.

An Intermediate Obstruction

Along our way to constructing the example in
Theorem

B
, we develop a new cohomological obstruction related to quadratic triples which only occurs in the purely characteristic
2
realm and is related to the behaviour of Lie algebra sheaves. In general, on any scheme
X
, given an Azumaya algebra with orthogonal involution
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
we can consider submodules of
\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}

\displaystyle=\Ker(\Id_{\mathcal{A}}-\sigma)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}

\displaystyle=\Ker(\Id_{\mathcal{A}}+\sigma)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}

\displaystyle=\Img(\Id_{\mathcal{A}}-\sigma)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}

\displaystyle=\Img(\Id_{\mathcal{A}}+\sigma)

called the symmetric, skew-symmetric, alternating, and symmetrized elements, respectively. If
\sigma
is a locally quadratic involution, meaning that
1\in\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(X)
, i.e., the unit of
\mathcal{A}(X)
is a symmetrized element, then there exist cohomological obstructions introduced in
[
GNR25
]

\displaystyle\Omega(\mathcal{A},\sigma)

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(X,\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})

\displaystyle\omega(\mathcal{A},\sigma)

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(X,\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})

called the strong and weak obstructions, respectively. These are defined as the images of
1\in\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(X)
under the boundary maps of the following long exact cohomology sequences (and so the strong obstruction maps to the weak obstruction under the rightmost vertical arrow).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X,\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X,\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})}

The weak obstruction controls whether
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
can be equipped with a semi-trace
f\colon\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
and the strong obstruction controls whether there exists a linear map
f^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
such that its restriction to the symmetric elements is a semi-trace.

Now, if we specialize to the purely characteristic
2
case, then

\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}=\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}\text{ and }\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}=\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}.

Furthermore, since we are assuming
1\in\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(X)=\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(X)
, this means that
\mathcal{O}_{X}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}\subseteq\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}
. Thus, we may add an intermediate row to the diagram above,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X,\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{O}_{X})(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{O}_{X})(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X,\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{O}_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(X,\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})}

and we call the image of
1
in
\mathrm{H}^{1}(X,\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{O}_{X})
the intermediate obstruction, denoted by
\Omega^{\prime}(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
. We show in
Section

2.C
that for any semi-trace
f
making
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
a quadratic triple, we have an exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\mathfrak{pgo}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow 0

where
\mathfrak{pgo}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}
is the Lie algebra sheaf of the automorphism group
\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}
. The intermediate obstruction, or more precisely its vanishing, controls whether such a sequence exists which is split exact (our
Corollary

2.9
).

Theorem C
.

Let
X
be a scheme over a field
k
of characteristic
2
and let
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
be an Azumaya algebra with locally quadratic orthogonal involution.

Then, the intermediate obstruction
\Omega^{\prime}(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
is zero if and only if there exists a semi-trace
f
making
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
a quadratic triple and such that sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}/\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\mathfrak{pgo}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow 0

is split exact.

While
Theorem

C
is not strictly needed for the proof of
Theorem

B
, we show how such results can naturally be obtained for arbitrary even degree Azumaya algebras by some cohomological arguments related to this sequence (see
Theorem

3.5
).

Organization

The contents of this paper are organized as follows. We recall definitions and basic properties of our main objects throughout the preliminaries section. Both Azumaya algebras and quadratic triples are addressed in
Section

1.A
. Their automorphism group schemes, which are linear algebraic groups, as well as the associated Lie algebra sheaves are discussed in
Section

1.B.ii
. The required details about the stack equivalence
A_{1}\times A_{1}\cong D_{2}
are covered in
Section

1.C
. The setup for what we consider a deformation problem is explained in
Section

1.D
, as well as background on canonical cohomological obstruction classes and classification of deformations. These can be neatly combined into a tangent-obstruction theory, discussed in
Section

1.D.iv
. We end the preliminaries by reviewing Igusa surfaces and their properties that are necessary for us in
Section

1.E
.

Our work on the intermediate obstruction in characteristic
2
is done in
Section

2
. Throughout this section we also discuss how the extension classes of various short exact sequences relate to the strong, intermediate, and weak obstructions, offering a new lens through which to view the two previously defined obstructions.

Relative obstructions are discussed in
Section

3
.

Finally, in
Section

4
we explicitly construct the example promised in
Theorem

B
. The two quaternion algebras
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
on an Igusa surface
X
come from concrete fppf
1
–cocycles. We use computations with these cocycles to verify that
\mathcal{Q}\otimes\mathcal{P}
has the required properties for our example. To see that the quadratic triple structure
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
on
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
coming from the equivalence
A_{1}\times A_{1}\cong D_{2}
does not deform, we transfer the question back to the
A_{1}\times A_{1}
side where one of
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
or
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
does not deform by construction, therefore obstructing the deformations of
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
. All of these details are assembled in
Theorem

4.5
.

1.
Preliminaries

1.A.
Azumaya algebras with additional structure

Throughout this section we fix a scheme
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
to be considered as a base.

1.A.i.
Azumaya algebras

Recall that an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is an
Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra
if there exists an fppf-cover
\{X_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
and finite locally free
\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}
-modules
\mathcal{M}_{i}
of everywhere positive rank such that there are isomorphisms
\mathcal{A}_{X_{i}}\cong\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}}(\mathcal{M}_{i})
for each
i\in I
. If each of the
\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}
-modules
\mathcal{M}_{i}
are, moreover, free, then we say that such a cover
splits
the Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
.

Due to
[
Mil80
, Theorem 3.9]
, for any Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
there is an étale cover of
X
that splits
\mathcal{A}
. This fact is useful for setting up a deformation theory for Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras equipped with additional structures. However, we frequently use splittings of Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebras along fppf-covers which are not étale.

1.A.ii.
Involutions on Azumaya algebras

Given an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, an involution of the first kind on
\mathcal{A}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module morphism
\sigma\colon\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
satisfying

(1)

\sigma^{2}=\Id_{\mathcal{A}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sigma(ab)=\sigma(b)\sigma(a)

for all sections
a,b\in\mathcal{A}(U)
for any open
U\subset X
. Since throughout this text we restrict ourselves to involutions of the first kind only (as opposed to involutions of the second kind, which need not be
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–linear), we will often implicitly assume that an involution is of the first kind without comment.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
be an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra with involution. There exists a sufficiently fine fppf-cover
\{X_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{X_{i}}\cong\Mat_{n_{i}}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}})
and the involution
\sigma_{X_{i}}
is adjoint to a regular bilinear form
b_{i}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}^{n_{i}}\times\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}^{n_{i}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}}
. The involution
\sigma
is called:

(i)

orthogonal
if all
b_{i}
are symmetric, i.e.,
b_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})=b_{i}(x_{2},x_{1})
,

(ii)

weakly symplectic
if all
b_{i}
are skew-symmetric, i.e.,
b_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})=-b_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})
,

(iii)

and
symplectic
if all
b_{i}
are alternating, i.e.,
b_{i}(x,x)=0
.

These notions are independent of the chosen cover and are thus well-defined globally. For an Azumaya algebra
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
with involution, we have two
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module morphisms
\Id_{\mathcal{A}}\pm\sigma\colon\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
and we define the following notable submodules of
\mathcal{A}
,

(2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}&=\Ker(\Id_{\mathcal{A}}-\sigma)\\
\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}&=\Ker(\Id_{\mathcal{A}}+\sigma)\\
\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}&=\Img(\Id_{\mathcal{A}}-\sigma)\\
\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}&=\Img(\Id_{\mathcal{A}}+\sigma)\end{split}

called the submodules of symmetric, skew-symmetric, alternating, and symmetrized elements respectively. For any open
U\subset X
, we can say that

(3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(U)&=\{a\in\mathcal{A}(U)\mid\sigma(a)=a\},\text{ and}\\
\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(U)&=\{a\in\mathcal{A}(U)\mid\sigma(a)=-a\}.\end{split}

However,
\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}
and
\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}
are image sheaves and in general do not have such convenient descriptions because their construction involves sheafification.

1.A.iii.
Quadratic triples

Orthogonal involutions considered on their own can be poorly behaved if
2
is not invertible over our base scheme
X
, so it is often advantageous to work with
quadratic pairs
. Taking our definition from
[
CF15
, 2.7.0.30]
and our terminology from
[
GNR25
, 3.1]
, for an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
we call
(\sigma,f)
a
quadratic pair on
\mathcal{A}
or call
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
a
quadratic triple
if:

(i)

\sigma
is an orthogonal involution on
\mathcal{A}
, and

(ii)

f\colon\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module morphism satisfying

f(a+\sigma(a))=\Trd_{\mathcal{A}}(a)

for all sections
a\in\mathcal{A}(U)
for all open
U\subset X
.

Here
\Trd_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
is the
reduced trace
of
\mathcal{A}
, which is the unique
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module morphism fppf locally isomorphic to the usual trace of matrix algebras. In the case that
2
is invertible over
X
, the second condition above forces
f=\frac{1}{2}\Trd_{\mathcal{A}}
. If this is the case, then we may simply ignore
f
as the theory of quadratic pairs coincides with the usual theory of orthogonal involutions. By
[
CF15
, 2.7.0.32]
, all quadratic triples are étale locally isomorphic, i.e., they are all twisted forms of one another.

Given an Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{A}
with an orthogonal involution
\sigma
on
\mathcal{A}
, it may not be possible to equip
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
with a semi-trace
f
to form a quadratic triple
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
. By
[
GNR25
, Lemma 3.14]
, one necessary condition for there to exist such an
f
is that the unit in
\mathcal{A}(X)
must be a symmetrized element, i.e.,
1\in\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(X)
. Orthogonal involutions which have this property are called
locally quadratic
since, by
[
GNR25
, Lemma 3.15]
, they may at least be equipped with semi-traces locally.

If
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
is an Azumaya algebra with locally quadratic involution, then we may describe all possible semi-traces
f
which make
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
a quadratic triple. First, since
\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}
lies in the kernel of the map
1+\sigma\colon\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}
, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

where
\xi
is the map induced by
1+\sigma
. Then,
[
GNR25
, Theorem 3.18]
states that there is a bijection between the set of possible semi-traces
f
and the set of global sections
\lambda\in(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})(X)
such that
\xi(\lambda)=1\in\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(X)
. For such a section
\lambda\in(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma})(X)
, there will exist a cover
\{X_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
over which
\lambda|_{X_{i}}
is the image of some section
\ell_{i}\in\mathcal{A}(X_{i})
with
\ell_{i}+\sigma(\ell_{i})=1
, and then the semi-trace
f
associated to
\lambda
is uniquely defined by the fact that

f|_{X_{i}}(s)=\Trd_{\mathcal{A}}(\ell_{i}\cdot s)

locally for any section
s\in\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(X_{i})
.

1.B.
Group schemes and Lie algebra sheaves

In the study of the deformation theory of Azumaya algebras, possibly with additional structure, both the automorphism group schemes associated to such objects and their Lie algebra sheaves play an important role. Here we review the definitions of these objects.

1.B.i.
Algebraic groups

Given any sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
X
, possibly with some additional structure (such as being an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–module, an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–algebra, etc.), the
automorphism sheaf

\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{F})
is the sheaf of groups on
X
is defined by

\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{F})(U)=\Aut(\mathcal{F}|_{U}),

for
U\subseteq X
open, i.e., this is the sheaf of internal automorphisms which respect any present additional structure. For Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–algebras and involutions we obtain the following familiar group sheaves.

(i)

The
projective general linear group
of
\mathcal{A}
is the group sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–algebra automorphisms
\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{A})
. We write
\mathbf{PGL}_{n}=\mathbf{PGL}_{\Mat_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{X})}
.

(ii)

If
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
is a quadratic triple, then the
projective general orthogonal group of
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
, denoted by
\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}=\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
, is the subgroup sheaf of
\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}
consisting of those automorphisms
\varphi
such that
\varphi\circ\sigma=\sigma\circ\varphi
and
f\circ\varphi=f
.

In the case that the quadratic triple is of the form
(\Mat_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\sigma_{2n},f_{2n})
, where
\sigma_{2n}
is the orthogonal involution

\sigma_{2n}(B)=\begin{bmatrix}&&1\\
&\iddots&\\
1&&\end{bmatrix}B^{T}\begin{bmatrix}&&1\\
&\iddots&\\
1&&\end{bmatrix},

which has the effect of reflecting a matrix
B\in\Mat_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
across the second diagonal, and the semi-trace
f_{2n}
is given by

f_{2n}\left(\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}&\ldots&*&*&\ldots&*\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
*&\ldots&a_{n}&*&\ldots&*\\
*&\ldots&*&a_{n}&\ldots&*\\
\vdots&&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
*&\ldots&*&*&\ldots&a_{1}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i},

then we write
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}
for
\mathbf{PGO}_{(\Mat_{2n}(\mathcal{O}_{X}),\sigma_{2n},f_{2n})}
.

By
[
CF15
, 2.4.4.1]
and
[
CF15
, 4.4.0.32]
(which are stated more generally over the fppf-site), these two group sheaves are represented by group schemes affine over
X
. Further, the representing group schemes are all smooth, and thus also flat, over
X
as this is true for their associated split forms. We use the same notation to refer to the representing group scheme as the sheaf, thus effectively extending the sheaf to the category
\mathsf{Sch}_{X}
via the usual convention that for
T\in\mathsf{Sch}_{X}
, we set

\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}(T)=\Hom_{\mathsf{Sch}_{X}}(T,\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}})

and likewise for the other groups. In general, if
\mathcal{F}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–module,
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–algebra, etc., and
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{F})
is represented by a group scheme, then for each scheme map
g\colon T\rightarrow X
there is a natural identification

\mathbf{G}(T)=\Aut_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}(g^{*}(\mathcal{F})).

We denote by
g^{*}(\mathbf{G})
, or
\mathbf{G}|_{T}
when
g
is clear, the restriction of
\mathbf{G}
to
T
, i.e., the
T
–group scheme represented by
\mathbf{G}\times_{X}T
. It has the property that for a scheme
T^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Sch}_{T}
with
X
-scheme structure
T^{\prime}\rightarrow T\rightarrow X
, we have

g^{*}(\mathbf{G})(T^{\prime})=\mathbf{G}(T^{\prime}).

One of the benefits of using quadratic pairs is that we obtain smooth representing group schemes. In characteristic
2
, if
\sigma
is an orthogonal involution which is not symplectic on an Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{A}
and, if we simply consider
\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
without a semi-trace, then, in general, the group sheaf
\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
is not representable by a smooth group scheme.

1.B.ii.
Lie algebra sheaves

Here we follow
[
CF15
, 2.8]
, adapting their approach using the fppf-site to our setting. A similar approach appears in
[
KMRT98
, §21.A]
.

Denote by
X[\varepsilon]
the
scheme of dual numbers
over
X
. By definition,
X[\varepsilon]
is the scheme with the same underlying topological space as
X
and with structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}[\varepsilon]\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}[x]/(x^{2})
, i.e.,
\varepsilon^{2}=0
. The canonical projection
\mathcal{O}_{X}[\varepsilon]\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
and its section
\mathcal{O}_{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}[\varepsilon]
define a closed immersion
i_{X}\colon X\rightarrow X[\varepsilon]
and a morphism
p_{X}\colon X[\varepsilon]\rightarrow X
respectively. We consider
X[\varepsilon]\in\mathsf{Sch}_{X}
with structure map
p_{X}
.

Note that, by construction, we have that
p_{X,*}(\mathcal{O}_{X[\varepsilon]})=\mathcal{O}_{X}[\varepsilon]
. Also, for each open subscheme
U\subseteq X
, there is a corresponding open subscheme
U[\varepsilon]\subseteq X[\varepsilon]
with the same topological space as
U
and with structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{U}[\varepsilon]
. The maps
i_{X}
and
p_{X}
restrict naturally to maps we call
i_{U}
and
p_{U}
.

Now let
\mathbf{G}
be a sheaf of groups on
X
represented by a group scheme over
X
. For each
U\subseteq X
open, we obtain a split exact sequence of groups

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ker(\mathbf{G}(i_{U}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{G}(U[\varepsilon])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{G}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{G}(i_{U})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{G}(p_{U})}

where
\mathbf{G}(i_{U})
is the restriction map

\mathbf{G}(U[\varepsilon])=\Hom_{\mathsf{Sch}_{X}}(U[\varepsilon],\mathbf{G})\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathsf{Sch}_{X}}(U,\mathbf{G})=\mathbf{G}(U)

induced by
i_{U}
, and likewise for
\mathbf{G}(p_{U})\colon\mathbf{G}(U)\rightarrow\mathbf{G}(U[\varepsilon])
. The group
\Ker(\mathbf{G}(i_{U}))
has the structure of a Lie algebra over
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
. According to
[
CF15
]
, these are the
U
points of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{Lie}_{\mathbf{G}}
of
\mathbf{G}
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{Lie}_{\mathbf{G}}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–module such that for all open
U\subset X
we have
\mathfrak{Lie}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)=\ker(\mathbf{G}(i_{U}))
. Alternatively, there is an exact sequence of group sheaves over
X
,

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Lie}_{\mathbf{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X*}(\mathbf{G}|_{X[\varepsilon]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}

where the maps
i^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}
are induced by the various
\mathbf{G}(i_{U})
and
\mathbf{G}(p_{U})
.

There is an equivalent definition of the Lie algebra sheaf of
\mathbf{G}
in terms of the module of differentials of the scheme
\mathbf{G}
over
X
. Let
p\colon\mathbf{G}\rightarrow X
be the structure map and
e\colon X\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
be the unit section. The module of relative differentials of
\mathbf{G}
over
X
is the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{G}}
–module
\Omega_{\mathbf{G}/X}=\Omega_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{G}}/p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{X})}
(see
[
Sta18
,
Tag 08RT(2)
]
for the definition). In
[
CF15
]
, Calmès and Fasel use the notation
\omega_{\mathbf{G}/X}^{1}=e^{*}(\Omega_{\mathbf{G}/X})
for this module pulled back to
X
along
e
. They then state in
[
CF15
, 2.8.0.38]
that the Lie algebra of
\mathbf{G}
is
\mathbf{W}\big((\omega_{\mathbf{G}/X}^{1})^{\vee}\big)
, using the
\mathbf{W}
functor of
[
DG70
, Exp I, 4.6.1]
to extend to the fppf-site. Working on a scheme as we do, we may simply write

(6)

\mathfrak{Lie}_{\mathbf{G}}=(e^{*}(\Omega_{\mathbf{G}/X})\big)^{\vee}.

So, for
U\subseteq X
open, we have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Lie}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{U}}(e^{*}(\Omega_{\mathbf{G}/X})|_{U},\mathcal{O}_{U}).

We now give concrete realizations of the Lie algebra sheaves for the group schemes
\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}
. These are given over fields in
[
KMRT98
, 23.A, 23.B]
and analogous descriptions hold here. If
\mathcal{A}
is an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–algebra, then
\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}|_{X[\varepsilon]}
is the group of
\mathcal{O}_{X[\varepsilon]}
–algebra automorphisms of
\mathcal{A}[\varepsilon]=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}[\varepsilon]
. The Lie algebra sheaf of
\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}
is then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{pgl}_{\mathcal{A}}\cong\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{O}_{X}

and it fits into (
5
) over some
U\subseteq X
open as in the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{pgl}_{\mathcal{A}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X*}(\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}|_{X[\varepsilon]})(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{O}_{X})(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut_{\mathcal{O}_{U[\varepsilon]}}(\mathcal{A}|_{U}[\varepsilon])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Inn(1+\varepsilon a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Here we view
\mathfrak{pgl}_{\mathcal{A}}
additively and the other groups multiplicatively. The Lie bracket is given by the commutator
[\overline{a},\overline{b}]=\overline{ab-ba}
. Since
\sigma(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, the involution descends to an involution
\overline{\sigma}
of
\mathfrak{pgl}_{\mathcal{A}}
, i.e., an order two
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–module isomorphism which satisfies
\overline{\sigma}([\overline{a},\overline{b}])=[\overline{\sigma}(\overline{b}),\overline{\sigma}(\overline{a})]
.

Remark 1.1
.

Alternatively, since
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{O}_{X}\cong\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A})
by the map
x\mapsto\ad(x)=(a\mapsto xa-ax)
sending a section
x
of
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{O}_{X}
to the associated adjoint derivation, one may view the Lie algebra sheaf
\mathfrak{pgl}_{\mathcal{A}}
in terms of derivations. In this case, the map
\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A})(U)\rightarrow\Aut_{\mathcal{O}_{U[\varepsilon]}}(\mathcal{A}|_{U}[\varepsilon])
sends a derivation
\Delta
to the map

a+\varepsilon b\mapsto a+\varepsilon b+\varepsilon\Delta(a).

If
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
is now a quadratic triple, then the Lie algebra sheaf of
\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}
is the preimage of the subgroup sheaf
p_{X*}(\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}|_{X[\varepsilon]})\subset p_{X*}(\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}|_{X[\varepsilon]})
under the map
\mathfrak{pgl}_{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow p_{X*}(\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{A}}|_{X[\varepsilon]})
given above. Namely,

\mathfrak{pgo}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}(U)=\{x\in\mathfrak{pgl}_{\mathcal{A}}(U)\mid x+\overline{\sigma}(x)=0,\;f\circ\ad(x)|_{\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.50694pt\textit{y\hskip-0.90417ptm}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma)}}=0\}

for
U\subseteq X
open.

1.C.
\mathfrak{A}_{1}\times\mathfrak{A}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{D}_{2}
, and the norm functor

Here we review the result
[
GNR24
, Theorem 5.13]
, which determines that there is an equivalence of stacks between the stack of quaternion algebras over a degree
2
étale cover of schemes and the stack of degree
4
quadratic triples. We have made some slight modifications to the notation since, in
[
GNR24
]
, the authors work with sheaves on the fppf-site. In this section, all schemes are considered over a fixed base field
k
.

First, the stack of quaternion algebras over degree
2
étale covers, denoted
\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{2}
or
\mathfrak{A}_{1}\times\mathfrak{A}_{1}
since it is related to algebraic groups of Dynkin type
A_{1}\times A_{1}
, has:

(i)

objects which are pairs
(T^{\prime}\rightarrow T,\mathcal{Q})
where
T^{\prime}\rightarrow T
is a degree
2
étale cover of
k
–schemes and
\mathcal{Q}
is a degree
2
(i.e., quaternion) Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{T^{\prime}}
–algebra on
T^{\prime}
,

(ii)

morphisms which are triples
(f,g,\varphi)\colon(T_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow T_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{1})\rightarrow(T_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrow T_{2},\mathcal{Q}_{2})
where
f\colon T_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow T_{2}^{\prime}
and
g\colon T_{1}\rightarrow T_{2}
are
k
–scheme morphisms making

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

a Cartesian diagram of schemes (so that
T_{1}^{\prime}
shares the universal property of
T_{1}\times_{T_{2}}T_{2}^{\prime}
) and where
\varphi\colon\mathcal{Q}_{1}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}f^{*}(\mathcal{Q}_{2})
is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{T_{1}^{\prime}}
–Azumaya algebras, and

(iii)

structure functor
p\colon\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{Sch}_{k}
defined by setting
p(T^{\prime}\rightarrow T,\mathcal{Q})=T
and
p(f,g,\varphi)=g
.

Here by
a degree
2
étale cover
, we mean the same as in
[
CF15
, 2.5.2]
: a
k
-morphism
f\colon T^{\prime}\rightarrow T
is an étale cover of degree
n
if
f
is an affine morphism and the sheaf
f_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{T^{\prime}})
is étale locally isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{T}^{n}
as
\mathcal{O}_{T}
–algebras (this description also follows from
[
Sta18
,
Tag 04HN
]
). Pullbacks in
\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{2}
can be taken as follows. For an arbitrary morphism
g\colon X\rightarrow T
in
\mathsf{Sch}_{k}
and an object
(T^{\prime}\rightarrow T,\mathcal{Q})\in\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{2}(T)
in the fiber over
T
, we get the fiber product diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{T}T^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where
X\times_{T}T^{\prime}\rightarrow X
is also a degree
2
étale cover since such maps are stable under base change. The morphism
(g^{\prime},g,\Id)\colon(X\times_{T}T^{\prime}\rightarrow X,g^{\prime*}(\mathcal{Q}))\rightarrow(T^{\prime}\rightarrow T,\mathcal{Q})
is cartesian, and thus
(X\times_{T}T^{\prime}\rightarrow X,g^{\prime*}(\mathcal{Q}))
is a pullback of
(T^{\prime}\rightarrow T,\mathcal{Q})
to
X
.

Second, the stack of degree
4
quadratic triples, denoted
\mathfrak{D}_{2}
because of its relation to groups of Dynkin type
D_{2}
, has

(i)

objects which are pairs
(T,(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f))
of a
k
-scheme
T
and a quadratic triple
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
on
T
such that the
\mathcal{O}_{T}
–Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{A}
has degree
4
,

(ii)

morphisms which are pairs
(g,\varphi)\colon(T_{1},(\mathcal{A}_{1},\sigma_{1},f_{1}))\rightarrow(T_{2},(\mathcal{A}_{2},\sigma_{2},f_{2}))
of a
k
-scheme morphism
g\colon T_{1}\rightarrow T_{2}
and an isomorphism
\varphi\colon(\mathcal{A}_{1},\sigma_{1},f_{1})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}g^{*}(\mathcal{A}_{2},\sigma_{2},f_{2})
of quadratic triples on
T_{1}
,

(iii)

and structure functor
p\colon\mathfrak{D}_{2}\rightarrow\mathsf{Sch}_{k}
defined by setting
p(T,(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f))=T
and
p(g,\varphi)=g
.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
GNR24
, 5.13]
)
.

There is an equivalence of stacks

\displaystyle N\colon\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{D}_{2}

\displaystyle(T^{\prime}\rightarrow T,\mathcal{Q})

\displaystyle\mapsto(T,(N_{T^{\prime}/T}(\mathcal{Q}),\sigma_{N},f_{N}))

induced by norm functors
N_{T^{\prime}/T}
associated to the finite étale extensions
T^{\prime}/T
.

We now review the explicit description of
(N_{T^{\prime}/T}(\mathcal{Q}),\sigma_{N},f_{N})
occuring above in the case when the étale extension
T^{\prime}\rightarrow T
is the split étale extension, i.e.
T\sqcup T\rightarrow T
. In this case, a quaternion
\mathcal{O}_{T\sqcup T}
–algebra is the data of two quaternion
\mathcal{O}_{T}
–algebras
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. Precisely, an open subset of
T\sqcup T
is of the form
U\sqcup V
for
U,V\subset T
open and

\mathcal{Q}(U\sqcup V)=\mathcal{Q}_{1}(U)\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}(V).

By
[
GNR24
, 3.11]
, the norm of such an algebra is simply the
\mathcal{O}_{T}
–algebra

N_{(T\sqcup T)/T}(\mathcal{Q})=\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}\mathcal{Q}_{2},

which is clearly degree
4
since each
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is degree
2
. To describe the involution
\sigma_{N}
, we first recall from
[
AFW19
, Example 2]
the fact
that every quaternion algebra
\mathcal{Q}
has a canonical (unique up to isomorphism) symplectic involution defined by

(7)

\psi\colon\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q},\quad\quad q\mapsto\Trd_{\mathcal{Q}}(q)\cdot 1-q.

Thus, the quaternion algebras
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
have their canonical symplectic involutions
\psi_{i}
and the tensor product

\sigma_{N}=\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}
of these involutions is an orthogonal involution on
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. Finally, the semi-trace
f_{N}
is described by
[
GNR25
, Proposition 4.6]
to be the unique semi-trace
f_{N}\colon\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{2},\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{T}
such that
f_{N}(s_{1}\otimes s_{2})=0
for all sections
s_{i}\in\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{Q}_{i},\psi_{i}}(U)
for all open
U\subset X
.

1.D.
Deformations and obstructions

Throughout this text we will consider a number of different “deformation situations”. The basic set-up is that we will have an object, a scheme, or a morphism of schemes and we will ask when such objects admit infinitesimal extensions. Such extensions will exist, for example, whenever our object is genuinely defined as the fiber of some family.

1.D.i.
Relative deformations

Throughout this section we consider two Artinian local
k
-algebras
(C,\mathfrak{m}_{C})
and
(C^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}_{C^{\prime}})
both having residue field
k
, i.e.
C/\mathfrak{m}_{C}\cong k
and
C^{\prime}/\mathfrak{m}_{C^{\prime}}\cong k
. We also fix a surjective
k
-algebra homomorphism
C^{\prime}\rightarrow C
with kernel an ideal
J\subset C^{\prime}
.

We are going to work in the following setting. We assume we are given

(i)

a scheme
X
over
k
,

(ii)

a scheme
{\widetilde{X}}
flat over
C
together with a closed immersion
i:X\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}
inducing an isomorphism
X\cong{\widetilde{X}}\times_{C}k
, and

(iii)

a scheme
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
flat over
C^{\prime}
together with a closed immersion
\tilde{i}:{\widetilde{X}}\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
inducing an isomorphism
{\widetilde{X}}\cong{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}\times_{C^{\prime}}C
.

In a diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(C^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}

where the vertical square faces are fiber product diagrams and we set
i_{0}=\tilde{i}\circ i
.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a coherent sheaf on
{\widetilde{X}}
flat over
C
. A deformation of
\mathcal{F}
to
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is a coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
on
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
flat over
C^{\prime}
together with an isomorphism
g\colon\tilde{i}^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{F}
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is an invertible sheaf (or a finite locally free sheaf), then we say that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is a deformation of an invertible sheaf (or of a finite locally free sheaf) if
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
has the same property as well.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Azumaya algebra on
{\widetilde{X}}
. A deformation of
\mathcal{A}
to
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
together with an isomorphism
g\colon\tilde{i}^{*}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{A}
of Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}}
-algebras.

Definition 1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an Azumaya algebra on
{\widetilde{X}}
and suppose that
\sigma
is an involution on
\mathcal{A}
. A deformation of the Azumaya algebra with involution
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
to
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is a pair
((\mathcal{A}^{\prime},g),\sigma^{\prime})
such that:

(i)

(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},g:\tilde{i}^{*}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{A})
is a deformation of
\mathcal{A}
to
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
as an Azumaya algebra,

(ii)

\sigma^{\prime}
is an involution on
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
making the diagram below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{i}^{*}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{i}^{*}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{i}^{*}\sigma^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

a commutative square. In particular,
\tilde{i}^{*}\sigma^{\prime}=g^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ g
.

Definition 1.6
.

Again, let
\mathcal{A}
be an Azumaya algebra on
{\widetilde{X}}
. Let
\sigma
be an involution on
\mathcal{A}
and suppose also that
\sigma
is part of a quadratic pair
(\sigma,f)
on
\mathcal{A}
. We say that a triple
((\mathcal{A}^{\prime},g),\sigma^{\prime},f^{\prime})
is a deformation to
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
of the Azumaya algebra with quadratic pair
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
if:

(i)

the pair
((\mathcal{A}^{\prime},g),\sigma^{\prime})
is a deformation of the Azumaya algebra with involution
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
and

(ii)

f^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
is a semi-trace such that the associated diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{i}^{*}\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{i}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{i}^{*}f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is commutative, where
g
restricts to a map between the respective symmetric elements since
\tilde{i}^{*}\sigma^{\prime}=g^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ g
.

Most of the time, we will be interested in deformations of sheaves with additional structures such as those listed in
1.3
to
1.6
. Occasionally though, we will also need a reference for deformations of certain types of morphisms of schemes. For this, let
Y
be any scheme flat over
C
.

Definition 1.7
.

Suppose that we are given an arbitrary
C
-morphism
f:Y\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}
. We say that a pair
((Y^{\prime},j:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}),f^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime})
is a deformation of the morphism
f
with target
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
if:

(i)

(Y^{\prime},j)
is a deformation of
Y
, i.e.
Y^{\prime}
is a flat
C^{\prime}
-scheme and
j
is a closed immersion inducing an isomorphism
Y\cong Y^{\prime}\times_{C^{\prime}}C
;

(ii)

f^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is a morphism giving an equality
\tilde{i}\circ f^{\prime}=f\circ j
.

1.D.ii.
Infinitesimal automorphisms

Here, we work with a deformation setup as above. In particular, since the surjection
C^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow C
is defined by the ideal
J\subset C^{\prime}
, the closed embedding
{\widetilde{X}}\hookrightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is defined by the quasi-coherent ideal
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}\otimes_{C^{\prime}}J
. Throughout this subsection, we make the additional assumption that
J\cdot\mathfrak{m}_{C^{\prime}}=0
. In particular, this implies that
J^{2}=0
and thus also
\mathcal{J}^{2}=0
.

Remark 1.8
.

Fix a field
k
. Let
(C^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}_{C^{\prime}})
and
(C,\mathfrak{m}_{C})
be two local Artinian
k
-algebras both with residue field
k
. We say that a surjection
C^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow C
of
k
-algebras with kernel
J\subset C^{\prime}
is a
small extension
if
J\cdot\mathfrak{m}_{C^{\prime}}=0
. This agrees with the definition of a small extension given in
[
FG05
, Definition 6.1.9]
.

There is another definition of a small extension, that Hartshorne gives in
[
Har10
, §16]
, which requires
\dim_{k}J=1
.

Any surjection of local Artinian
k
-algebras with residue field
k
can be factored into a sequence of small extensions by Nakayama’s lemma. Further, any small extension of local Artinian
k
-algebras
(J\subset C^{\prime},C)
, as above, can be factored into a sequence of small extensions with
1
-dimensional kernel by choosing a basis for
J
. So, there is not much loss of generality choosing between the two definitions.

Note that, since
{\widetilde{X}}\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is an infinitesimal thickening, both schemes have the same underlying topological space. Thus, for each open subscheme
U\subseteq{\widetilde{X}}
, there is a corresponding open subscheme
U^{\prime}\subseteq{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
and an infinitesimal thickening
i_{U}\colon U\hookrightarrow U^{\prime}
defined by the ideal
\mathcal{J}|_{U^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{U^{\prime}}
.

We assume we have a sheaf
{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime}
on
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
such that
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}=\mathbf{Aut}({\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime})
is represented by a group scheme over
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
. For example,
{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime}
may be a finite locally free
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
–module, an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
-algebra, or an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
–algebra with additional structure. We set
{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=\tilde{i}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{F}=i^{*}(\tilde{i}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime}))
and denote their automorphism groups
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
and
\mathbf{G}
respectively. Thus,
{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime}
is a deformation of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}
. Note that for
U\subseteq{\widetilde{X}}
open, we have that
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(U)={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}(U)
, as well as similar relations for open sets in
X
.

Since the pullbacks we consider are functorial, for each open subscheme
U\subseteq{\widetilde{X}}
and automorphism
\varphi\colon{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime}|_{U^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime}|_{U^{\prime}}
, there is a pullback of this morphism

\tilde{i}_{U}^{*}(\varphi)\colon\tilde{i}_{U}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime}|_{U})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\tilde{i}_{U}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime}|_{U})

which defines an automorphism of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}|_{U}
. Thus, we obtain a restriction map

{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(U^{\prime})\rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(U)={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}(U).

Alternatively, this restriction map is simply the morphism
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(\tilde{i}_{U})
induced by the closed embedding
\tilde{i}_{U}\colon U\rightarrow U^{\prime}
. An
infinitesimal automorphism of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{\prime}
over
U^{\prime}
is an element in the kernel of this restriction map. We also have a restriction morphism of group sheaves on
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
,

(8)

\res\colon{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{i}_{*}(\tilde{i}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}))

where over any
U^{\prime}\subseteq{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
, we identify

\tilde{i}_{*}(\tilde{i}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}))(U^{\prime})=\tilde{i}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime})(U)={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(U)

and
\res(U)\colon{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(U^{\prime})\rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(U)
is the restriction map
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(\tilde{i}_{U})
. We wish to describe the kernel of this restriction morphism, which will be a group sheaf of infinitesimal automorphisms.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a commutative
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–algebra and let
\mathcal{M}
be an
\mathcal{R}
–module. Consider the sheaf
\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{M})
on
X
defined over
U\subseteq X
by

\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{M})(U)=\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{U}}(\mathcal{R}|_{U},\mathcal{M}|_{U})

where a derivation
d\colon\mathcal{R}|_{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}|_{U}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{U}
–module morphism satisfying the Liebniz rule
d(ab)=ad(b)+d(a)b
for appropriate sections
a,b
of
\mathcal{R}|_{U}
. The sheaf
\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{M})
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
–module.

Proposition 1.9
.

Let
\tilde{i}\colon{\widetilde{X}}\hookrightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
be a closed immersion defined by a square-zero quasi-coherent ideal
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
. Let
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
be a group scheme over
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
. Let
p\colon{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
and
e\colon{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
be the structure morphism and identity section of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
, respectively. Consider
e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})
as an
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
–algebra via the map
e^{-1}(p^{\sharp})\colon\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}\rightarrow e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})
and consider
\mathcal{J}
as an
e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})
–module via
e^{\sharp}\colon e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
.

Then, the kernel of the restriction map
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i)\colon{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{X}}^{\prime})\rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{X}})
is isomorphic to the group of derivations
\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})

Proof.

Because
\mathcal{J}
is square-zero, the inclusion
i\colon{\widetilde{X}}\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is a homeomorphism on the underlying topological spaces and we can consider
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}}=\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}/\mathcal{J}
. The identity in
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{X}})
is the morphism
{\widetilde{X}}\xrightarrow{e\circ i}{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
, so an element in the kernel of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i)
is a morphism
X^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{Sch}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
, which must be a section of
p
, making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\circ i}

commute. Because
i
is a homeomophism on the underlying spaces, the topological data of such a morphism is uniquely determined and so we only need to care about the morphisms of sheaves on
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
making the following diagram commute.

(9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}/\mathcal{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e\circ i)^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\sharp}}

Of course,
e^{\sharp}\colon e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
is one possible such morphism. Let
\psi\colon e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
be another such morphism. Since
\psi
must define a section of
p
, we have that
\psi\circ e^{-1}(p^{\sharp})=\Id_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}
. Letting
x
and
y
be sections of
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
and
e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})
respectively, and recalling that the module structure is
x\cdot y=e^{-1}(p^{\sharp})(x)y
by definition, we have

\psi(x\cdot y)=\psi(e^{-1}(p^{\sharp})(x)y)=x\psi(y),

which shows that
\psi
is
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
–linear.

We claim that
e^{\sharp}-\psi
is a section of
\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})
. It is clear that
e^{\sharp}-\psi
is
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
–linear and that
(e^{\sharp}-\psi)(y)
is a section of
\mathcal{J}
for all sections
y
of
e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})
. We also note that this means
e^{\sharp}(y)j=\psi(y)j
for any
j
of
\mathcal{J}
since
\mathcal{J}
is square-zero. Now, we compute for sections
y_{1},y_{2}
of
\mathcal{J}
:

\displaystyle(e^{\sharp}-\psi)(y_{1}y_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\sharp}(y_{1}y_{2})-\psi(y_{1}y_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\sharp}(y_{1})e^{\sharp}(y_{2})-\psi(y_{1})\psi(y_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\sharp}(y_{1})e^{\sharp}(y_{2})-e^{\sharp}(y_{1})\psi(y_{2})+e^{\sharp}(y_{1})\psi(y_{2})-\psi(y_{1})\psi(y_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\sharp}(y_{1})(e^{\sharp}-\psi)(y_{2})+(e^{\sharp}-\psi)(y_{1})\psi(y_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\sharp}(y_{1})(e^{\sharp}-\psi)(y_{2})+(e^{\sharp}-\psi)(y_{1})e^{\sharp}(y_{2})

\displaystyle=y_{1}\cdot(e^{\sharp}-\psi)(y_{2})+(e^{\sharp}-\psi)(y_{1})\cdot y_{2}

in the
e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})
–module
\mathcal{J}
. This show that
e^{\sharp}-\psi\colon e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
is a derivation.

Conversely, let
d
be a derivation from
\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})
. If
e^{\sharp}+d
is a homomorphism of sheaves of rings, then it is clear that it may take the place of the dashed morphism in (
9
) above. Hence, we compute

\displaystyle(e^{\sharp}+d)(y_{1})(e^{\sharp}+d)(y_{2})

\displaystyle=(e^{\sharp}(y_{1})+d(y_{1}))(e^{\sharp}(y_{2})+d(y_{2}))

\displaystyle=e^{\sharp}(y_{1})e^{\sharp}(y_{2})+e^{\sharp}(y_{1})d(y_{2})+d(y_{1})e^{\sharp}(y_{2})+d(y_{1})d(y_{2})

\displaystyle=e^{\sharp}(y_{1}y_{2})+y_{1}\cdot d(y_{2})+d(y_{1})\cdot y_{2}+0

\displaystyle=e^{\sharp}(y_{1}y_{2})+d(y_{1}y_{2})

\displaystyle=(e^{\sharp}+d)(y_{1}y_{2})

which shows that
e^{\sharp}+d
is a ring sheaf homomorphism as required.

So far, we have shown that we have a bijection of sets

\Ker({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i))\leftrightarrow\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J}).

It remains to show that this is a group isomorphism. In particular, we argue that multiplication in
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{X}}^{\prime})
between elements in the kernel of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i)
corresponds to addition of derivations. We note that the following is inspired by the approach in
[
DG70
, Exposé 2]
where they treat the case when
\mathcal{J}
is a direct summand of
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
.

We consider an additional scheme with the same underlying topological space as
|{\widetilde{X}}|=|{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}|
. First, we define a ring structure on the
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
–module
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}\oplus\mathcal{J}
by

(x_{1},j_{1})(x_{2},j_{2})=(x_{1}x_{2},x_{1}j_{2}+x_{2}j_{1}),

i.e., the direct summand
\mathcal{J}
is a second square-zero copy of
\mathcal{J}
in addition to the one which is a submodule
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
. We denote by
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}[\mathcal{J}]
the space
|{\widetilde{X}}|
equipped with this sheaf of rings. We have a closed embedding
i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}\colon{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}[\mathcal{J}]
defined by the square-zero ideal
0\oplus\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}\oplus\mathcal{J}
. Furthermore, this embedding is split by the map
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}\colon{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}[\mathcal{J}]\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
defined by the canonical inclusion
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}\oplus\mathcal{J}
. Both
i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}
are homeomorphisms by construction. The identity section in
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}[\mathcal{J}])
is the map
e\circ\pi_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}\colon{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}[\mathcal{J}]\rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
.

We now consider the diagram

(10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}[\mathcal{J}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\circ i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

and seek to identify which dashed morphisms
\varphi
belong to
\Ker({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}\circ i))
, i.e., such that
\varphi\circ i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}\circ i=e\circ i
. Because both horizontal maps along the bottom are homeomorphisms on the underlying topological spaces, given such a
\varphi
we would have
\varphi^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})=e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})
on
|{\widetilde{X}}|
. Thus, a suitable morphism
\varphi
is determined by a map of sheaves of rings
\varphi^{\sharp}
making

(11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}/\mathcal{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}\oplus\mathcal{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e\circ i)^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime\sharp}}

commute. In particular,
i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime\sharp}\circ\varphi^{\sharp}
must belong to
\Ker({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i))=\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})
identified above. Therefore, we may write

\varphi^{\sharp}(y)=(\psi(y),f(y))

for
y
a section of
e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})
where
\psi=e^{\sharp}+d
for some
d
of
\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})
. We claim that
f
is also such a derivation. Since
\varphi^{\sharp}
is a ring homomorphism, we can compute, for sections
y_{1},y_{2}
of
e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}})
,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\sharp}(y_{1}y_{2})

\displaystyle=(\psi(y_{1}),f(y_{1}))\cdot(\psi(y_{2}),f(y_{2}))

\displaystyle=(\psi(y_{1})\psi(y_{2}),\psi(y_{1})f(y_{2})+\psi(y_{2})f(y_{1}))

\displaystyle=(\psi(y_{1}y_{2}),e^{\sharp}(y_{1})f(y_{2})+e^{\sharp}(y_{2})f(y_{1}))

since the
f(y_{i})
are contained in
\mathcal{J}
. Thus,
f(y_{1}y_{2})=e^{\sharp}(y_{1})f(y_{2})+e^{\sharp}(y_{2})f(y_{1})
, which shows that
f\in\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})
. Conversely, it is clear that any such derivation can occur in the second factor. Therefore, we have a bijection of sets

\Ker({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}\circ i))=\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})\times\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J}).

Next, we consider the morphism of schemes
\sigma\colon{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}[\mathcal{J}]
defined by the map

\displaystyle\sigma^{\sharp}\colon\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}\oplus\mathcal{J}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle(x,j)

\displaystyle\mapsto x+j

which is a homomorphism of sheaves of rings since the copy of
\mathcal{J}
in
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
is square-zero. The induced morphism
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(\sigma)\colon{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}[\mathcal{J}])\rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}({\widetilde{X}}^{\prime})
restricts to the kernels discussed above, and takes the form

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(\sigma)\colon\Ker({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}\circ i))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Ker({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i))

\displaystyle(e^{\sharp}+d,f)

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{\sharp}+(d+f)

since on the square-zero ideals,
\sigma^{\sharp}
is simply the module addition
\mathcal{J}\oplus\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
. In other words, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ker({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i_{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}\circ i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})\times\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ker({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}

where we highlight the fact that
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}(\sigma)
is a homomorphism with respect to the group structure within
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
since, as a sheaf,
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
is a group functor. In particular, this equips
\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})
with two group structures, one of which is defined by a homomorphism with respect to the other. Thus,
[
DG70
, Exp 2, 3.10]
applies and we conclude that the group structures coincide (and furthermore are commutative, but this is apparent for addition of derivations). This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 1.10
.

Working in the same setting as
Proposition

1.9
, the kernel of the restriction morphism

\res\colon{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{i}_{*}(\tilde{i}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}))

of (
8
) is the sheaf
\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})
.

Proof.

Denote the kernel of the morphism
\res\colon{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\tilde{i}_{*}(\tilde{i}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}))
by
\mathcal{K}
. Let
U^{\prime}\subseteq{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
be an open subset. We have a fiber product diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\times_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

where
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\times_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}U^{\prime}
represents the restriction of the sheaf
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
to
U^{\prime}
. Additionally, the closed embedding
\tilde{i}_{U}\colon U\rightarrow U^{\prime}
from the reduction
U=U^{\prime}\times_{{\widetilde{X}}}{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is defined by the square-zero ideal
\mathcal{J}|_{U^{\prime}}
. Therefore, applying
Proposition

1.9
we see that

\mathcal{K}(U^{\prime})=\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{U^{\prime}}}(e_{U}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\times_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}U^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J}|_{U^{\prime}}).

However, since
U^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is an open immersion, so is
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\times_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}U^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
, and therefore
\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\times_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}U^{\prime}}=\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}|_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\times_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}U^{\prime}}
. Thus, we have that
e_{U}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\times_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}U^{\prime}})=e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})|_{U^{\prime}}
, and therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(U^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Der_{\mathcal{O}_{U^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}})|_{U^{\prime}},\mathcal{J}|_{U^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})(U^{\prime}).

This means that
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
i_{0}\colon X\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
be a closed embedding of schemes defined by a nilpotent ideal
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}
. Additionally, we consider

(i)

an ideal
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{M}\cdot\mathcal{J}=0
, and

(ii)

a group scheme
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
over
{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
such that the structure morphism
p^{\prime}\colon{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}
is flat.

Let
e^{\prime}\colon{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
be the identity section. Set
\mathbf{G}={\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}\times_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}X
to be the restriction of
{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}
to
X
, with identity section
e\colon X\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
. Then, there is a canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}}}(e^{\prime-1}(\mathcal{O}_{{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{J})\cong\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{e\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(e^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{G}}),\mathcal{J})

on the topological space
|{\widetilde{X}}^{\prime}|=|X|
.

Proof.
